========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doc, it's... it's kind of embarrassing, honestly, but I guess that's why I'm here. See, I work these late shifts at the plant, right? And by the time I clock out, it's dark, I'm exhausted, my feet are killing me, and the only thing open is the drive-thru. So, I'll stop to get something to eat because I haven't had a real break in hours. But it's never just a quick bite. I'll order, like, two double burgers, a large fry, maybe one of those extra-large milkshakes. And I sit in my truck and just... inhale it. 

But that's not even the worst part. You'd think after all that food, I'd be stuffed, right? Ready to just hit the mattress. But I get home, and it's like I haven't eaten a thing all day. I'll go straight out to the extra fridge we keep in the garage—where my wife puts all the leftovers and the bulk groceries—and I just start eating whatever is in there. Cold pasta, half a loaf of bread, whatever. It's like a whole second supper, right on top of the takeout. And I don't even really taste it after a while, I just can't seem to stop myself once I start. It's been happening a lot lately, and it's just... it's really getting out of hand, you know? I just don't know why I keep doing it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah... guilty doesn't even really begin to cover it, doc. It's more like... I just feel completely disgusted with myself. You know, I've always thought of myself as a pretty disciplined guy. Dependable, you know? I've been at the plant for years, I work hard, I provide for my family, I show up for my shifts. If there's a job to do, I buckle down and I do it. I'm just not the type to lose control. 

But then... then I'm standing out there in the cold garage at one in the morning, in my socks, scraping the bottom of a Tupperware container, and the reality of what I just did hits me. I feel so ashamed. It's like I'm sneaking around my own house, hiding from my own wife. I'm a 54-year-old man sneaking around like some kid who broke curfew, just so I can stuff my face in the dark. It really makes me feel like I'm letting myself down. I mean, how can a guy who prides himself on being so reliable just throw all his self-control out the window the second he gets home? I just beat myself up over it the whole next day, going over and over it in my head, trying to figure out why I'm so weak when it comes to this... it just really eats at a guy, you know? Feeling like you can't even trust yourself in your own kitchen.

That is exactly what it is, doc. That might actually be the scariest part of this whole thing for me. I'll be standing out there—like I said, by that old Kenmore fridge we keep in the garage, the one that hums real loud—and the cold air is just hitting my chest. I'll pull out, say, a family-size bag of chips, or maybe a box of those frosted cookies my wife buys for when the grandkids come over on Sundays. And I'll take that first bite, thinking, you know, just a taste to settle my stomach after the burgers. 

But once I start... it feels like a switch is flipped in my brain. It's the strangest, most frustrating thing. I'll be standing there, chewing, and my own mind is practically screaming at me. I'm literally telling myself, plain as day, "Roy, shut the bag and go inside. Just roll the top down, put the plastic clip back on, turn off the garage light, and go to bed." I'm having this whole rational conversation with myself in my head. 

But it's like my body isn't even listening to me anymore. My hands just keep reaching for more. Hand to bag, hand to mouth, over and over. I'll literally watch my own hand go back into the bag, pull out another handful, and I just keep eating. Even while I'm standing there thinking about how much I'm going to regret it when my alarm goes off at 5 AM for my next shift, I just can't make my hand stop. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body. I used to hear people say things like that and think, well, that's just an excuse for having no willpower. But I'm telling you, I am right there, fully aware, begging myself to just shut the door and walk away, and I just can't do it until the food is completely gone or I feel so stuffed I can't breathe right. It's a terrifying feeling, losing the steering wheel to your own self like that.

And I don't even really taste it after a while, I just can't seem to stop myself once I start. It's been happening a lot lately, and it's just... it's really getting out of hand, you know? I just don't know why I keep doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah... guilty doesn't even really begin to cover it, doc. It's more like... I just feel completely disgusted with myself. You know, I've always thought of myself as a pretty disciplined guy. Dependable, you know? I've been at the plant for years, I work hard, I provide for my family, I show up for my shifts. If there's a job to do, I buckle down and I do it. I'm just not the type to lose control. \n\nBut then... then I'm standing out there in the cold garage at one in the morning, in my socks, scraping the bottom of a Tupperware container, and the reality of what I just did hits me. I feel so ashamed. It's like I'm sneaking around my own house, hiding from my own wife. I'm a 54-year-old man sneaking around like some kid who broke curfew, just so I can stuff my face in the dark. It really makes me feel like I'm letting myself down. I mean, how can a guy who prides himself on being so reliable just throw all his self-control out the window the second he gets home? I just beat myself up over it the whole next day, going over and over it in my head, trying to figure out why I'm so weak when it comes to this... it just really eats at a guy, you know? Feeling like you can't even trust yourself in your own kitchen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you start during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: That is exactly what it is, doc. That might actually be the scariest part of this whole thing for me. I'll be standing out there—like I said, by that old Kenmore fridge we keep in the garage, the one that hums real loud—and the cold air is just hitting my chest. I'll pull out, say, a family-size bag of chips, or maybe a box of those frosted cookies my wife buys for when the grandkids come over on Sundays. And I'll take that first bite, thinking, you know, just a taste to settle my stomach after the burgers. \n\nBut once I start... it feels like a switch is flipped in my brain. It's the strangest, most frustrating thing. I'll be standing there, chewing, and my own mind is practically screaming at me. I'm literally telling myself, plain as day, \"Roy, shut the bag and go inside. Just roll the top down, put the plastic clip back on, turn off the garage light, and go to bed.\" I'm having this whole rational conversation with myself in my head. \n\nBut it's like my body isn't even listening to me anymore. My hands just keep reaching for more. Hand to bag, hand to mouth, over and over. I'll literally watch my own hand go back into the bag, pull out another handful, and I just keep eating. Even while I'm standing there thinking about how much I'm going to regret it when my alarm goes off at 5 AM for my next shift, I just can't make my hand stop. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body. I used to hear people say things like that and think, well, that's just an excuse for having no willpower. But I'm telling you, I am right there, fully aware, begging myself to just shut the door and walk away, and I just can't do it until the food is completely gone or I feel so stuffed I can't breathe right. It's a terrifying feeling, losing the steering wheel to your own self like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, absolutely. Fast doesn't even... I mean, it's like a race against the clock, doc. When I'm sitting in the cab of my truck after the drive-thru, or later when I'm out in the workshop by that garage fridge, I am just shoveling it in. I eat so fast that, honestly, half the time I barely even taste what I'm eating. 

You'd think if a guy is going to buy all that food, he'd want to sit back and enjoy it, right? Savor it a little bit after a twelve-hour shift on the line. But no, I'm just tearing into the wrappers and practically swallowing it whole. It's this... this frantic energy. I'll be out there in the workshop with whatever I grabbed, and I'm just cramming it down my throat like I’m trying to get it all down before somebody catches me. 

Which is crazy when you think about it, because who's going to catch me? My wife is a heavy sleeper, she's been in bed for hours. But my heart is pounding, and I'm looking over my shoulder at the door to the house, just eating as fast as humanly possible. It's almost like I'm a stray dog that found a scrap in the alley and has to gulp it down before a bigger dog comes along and snatches it away. I don't even chew properly, I just wash it down and keep going. It's this incredible rush to get it over with and hide the evidence, even while I'm still in the middle of doing it.

It's like I'm a passenger in my own body. I used to hear people say things like that and think, well, that's just an excuse for having no willpower. I'll be out there in the workshop with whatever I grabbed, and I'm just cramming it down my throat like I’m trying to get it all down before somebody catches me. \n\nWhich is crazy when you think about it, because who's going to catch me? My wife is a heavy sleeper, she's been in bed for hours. But my heart is pounding, and I'm looking over my shoulder at the door to the house, just eating as fast as humanly possible. It's almost like I'm a stray dog that found a scrap in the alley and has to gulp it down before a bigger dog comes along and snatches it away. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, if I really sit down and look at it... for the past few months, it’s been happening a lot more than I’d like to admit. I’d say there’s usually at least one night a week, guaranteed, where I end up doing this. But if I'm being completely honest with myself—and with you—it’s often two or three nights a week. 

It almost always hits me after a late shift, or, you know, a really rough day on the line. The schedule at the plant has been absolutely brutal lately, doc. They’ve got us doing these mandatory overtime blocks, and when the machinery acts up—like last week, the main conveyor belt on line three kept seizing up—the foreman just gets so wound up, and that tension just trickles right down to all of us on the floor. You're standing there on the concrete for ten, twelve hours, just trying to keep the quota up. 

By the time I finally punch out, my back is aching, my shoulders are practically up to my ears, and my brain is just totally fried. I'll get in my truck and I try to tell myself, 'Roy, just drive straight home, take a hot shower, and get right into bed. You need the sleep.' But then the stress of the day just sort of... settles into my bones on the drive back, you know? And before I know it, I'm pulling into the driveway, but I'm not going inside to my wife and my bed. I'm heading straight for the workshop and that garage fridge. 

So yeah, it's been a steady two or three times a week for a few months now, just depending on how bad the shifts are. You tell yourself it's just a one-time thing to blow off steam because work was a nightmare, but then Tuesday becomes Thursday, and Thursday becomes Friday, and suddenly the weeks just start piling up and you realize it's become a whole routine.

I'll be standing out there—like I said, by that old Kenmore fridge we keep in the garage, the one that hums real loud—and the cold air is just hitting my chest. I'll pull out, say, a family-size bag of chips, or maybe a box of those frosted cookies my wife buys for when the grandkids come over on Sundays. And I'll take that first bite, thinking, you know, just a taste to settle my stomach after the burgers. \n\nBut once I start... it feels like a switch is flipped in my brain. It's the strangest, most frustrating thing. I'll be standing there, chewing, and my own mind is practically screaming at me. I'm literally telling myself, plain as day, \"Roy, shut the bag and go inside. Just roll the top down, put the plastic clip back on, turn off the garage light, and go to bed.\" I'm having this whole rational conversation with myself in my head. \n\nBut it's like my body isn't even listening to me anymore. My hands just keep reaching for more. Hand to bag, hand to mouth, over and over. I'll literally watch my own hand go back into the bag, pull out another handful, and I just keep eating. Even while I'm standing there thinking about how much I'm going to regret it when my alarm goes off at 5 AM for my next shift, I just can't make my hand stop. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body. I used to hear people say things like that and think, well, that's just an excuse for having no willpower. But I'm telling you, I am right there, fully aware, begging myself to just shut the door and walk away, and I just can't do it until the food is completely gone or I feel so stuffed I can't breathe right. It's a terrifying feeling, losing the steering wheel to your own self like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Fast doesn't even... I mean, it's like a race against the clock, doc. When I'm sitting in the cab of my truck after the drive-thru, or later when I'm out in the workshop by that garage fridge, I am just shoveling it in. I eat so fast that, honestly, half the time I barely even taste what I'm eating. \n\nYou'd think if a guy is going to buy all that food, he'd want to sit back and enjoy it, right? Savor it a little bit after a twelve-hour shift on the line. But no, I'm just tearing into the wrappers and practically swallowing it whole. It's this... this frantic energy. I'll be out there in the workshop with whatever I grabbed, and I'm just cramming it down my throat like I’m trying to get it all down before somebody catches me. \n\nWhich is crazy when you think about it, because who's going to catch me? My wife is a heavy sleeper, she's been in bed for hours. But my heart is pounding, and I'm looking over my shoulder at the door to the house, just eating as fast as humanly possible. It's almost like I'm a stray dog that found a scrap in the alley and has to gulp it down before a bigger dog comes along and snatches it away. I don't even chew properly, I just wash it down and keep going. It's this incredible rush to get it over with and hide the evidence, even while I'm still in the middle of doing it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit down and look at it... for the past few months, it’s been happening a lot more than I’d like to admit. I’d say there’s usually at least one night a week, guaranteed, where I end up doing this. But if I'm being completely honest with myself—and with you—it’s often two or three nights a week. \n\nIt almost always hits me after a late shift, or, you know, a really rough day on the line. The schedule at the plant has been absolutely brutal lately, doc. They’ve got us doing these mandatory overtime blocks, and when the machinery acts up—like last week, the main conveyor belt on line three kept seizing up—the foreman just gets so wound up, and that tension just trickles right down to all of us on the floor. You're standing there on the concrete for ten, twelve hours, just trying to keep the quota up. \n\nBy the time I finally punch out, my back is aching, my shoulders are practically up to my ears, and my brain is just totally fried. I'll get in my truck and I try to tell myself, 'Roy, just drive straight home, take a hot shower, and get right into bed. You need the sleep.' But then the stress of the day just sort of... settles into my bones on the drive back, you know? And before I know it, I'm pulling into the driveway, but I'm not going inside to my wife and my bed. I'm heading straight for the workshop and that garage fridge. \n\nSo yeah, it's been a steady two or three times a week for a few months now, just depending on how bad the shifts are. You tell yourself it's just a one-time thing to blow off steam because work was a nightmare, but then Tuesday becomes Thursday, and Thursday becomes Friday, and suddenly the weeks just start piling up and you realize it's become a whole routine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

You tell yourself it's just a one-time thing to blow off steam because work was a nightmare, but then Tuesday becomes Thursday, and Thursday becomes Friday, and suddenly the weeks just start piling up and you realize it's become a whole routine.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you usually feel guilty or upset after these late-night eating episodes?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / production_worker / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Negative_Affect_After_Binge\nmanifestation: \"Afterward I feel ashamed and kind of disgusted with myself, because I’ve always thought of myself as disciplined and dependable, and this makes me feel like I’m sneaking around and letting myself down.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Guilty? Oh, doc, guilty doesn't even begin to cover it. It's... it's this heavy, sinking feeling that just drops right into the pit of my stomach the second I take that last bite, or the second I finally force myself to stop. I'll be standing there in the cold garage, looking down at the empty chip bags, or the crumbs from those frosted cookies scattered all over the workbench, and this wave of... just absolute disgust washes over me. I feel so ashamed, I really do. 

You see, I've worked at the plant for over twenty years now, right? Started on the floor right out of community college when I realized a desk job wasn't for me. And in all those years, I've built a reputation. Ask anyone on line three, ask the foreman—they'll tell you Roy is the guy you can count on. I show up early, I stay late, I don't complain, I get the job done. I've always prided myself on being disciplined. Being a dependable man, a provider for my wife, someone my grandkids can look up to. That's who I am. Or at least, that's who I thought I was.

But then I look at what I've just done... sneaking around in my own garage in the middle of the night, cramming food into my mouth like I'm out of my mind, hiding the wrappers at the bottom of the trash can so my wife doesn't see them when she takes the bags out in the morning. It makes me feel like a fraud. Like I'm living this double life. Here I am, this dependable guy during the day, but at night I'm sneaking around like a teenager trying not to get caught breaking curfew. I'm letting myself down, doc. Every single time I do it, I tell myself, 'Never again, Roy, that was the last time.' And then a few days later, I'm right back out there, doing it again, and the shame just piles up higher and higher until I feel like I can barely look at myself in the mirror when I'm shaving the next morning. It's just... it's a terrible way to live with yourself.

It's the strangest, most frustrating thing. I'll be standing there, chewing, and my own mind is practically screaming at me. I'm literally telling myself, plain as day, \"Roy, shut the bag and go inside. Just roll the top down, put the plastic clip back on, turn off the garage light, and go to bed.\" I'm having this whole rational conversation with myself in my head. \n\nBut it's like my body isn't even listening to me anymore. My hands just keep reaching for more. Hand to bag, hand to mouth, over and over. I'll literally watch my own hand go back into the bag, pull out another handful, and I just keep eating. Even while I'm standing there thinking about how much I'm going to regret it when my alarm goes off at 5 AM for my next shift, I just can't make my hand stop. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body. I used to hear people say things like that and think, well, that's just an excuse for having no willpower. But I'm telling you, I am right there, fully aware, begging myself to just shut the door and walk away, and I just can't do it until the food is completely gone or I feel so stuffed I can't breathe right. It's a terrifying feeling, losing the steering wheel to your own self like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about five months, since I was put in the acting lead role on the CNC line.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / production_worker / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S049\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Control? No. No, not at all. That's the scariest part of the whole thing, doc. It's like... well, it's exactly like someone else takes the wheel, you know? I'll be out there, standing by the workbench, and maybe I just went out to grab a bottle of water or put away a tool I left out from the weekend. But then I open that fridge, or I see the bag of chips I stashed behind the paint cans, and it's like... click. A switch just flips in my brain. 

Once I start, once I take that first bite, the control is just gone. Completely gone. I'll actually be standing there, chewing, and my own mind is screaming at me. I'm literally telling myself, 'Roy, shut the bag. Just roll the top down, put it away, and go inside. Go to bed.' I hear the voice clear as day. But my hands... my hands just aren't listening to me. They just keep reaching into the bag, pulling out more, shoving it in. 

It's the strangest, most helpless feeling in the world. You're a grown man, you've worked hard your whole life, you pride yourself on discipline, like I said before. I can stand on that factory floor for twelve hours and not take a break until the whistle blows, because I have control over my body and my work. But put me in front of that garage fridge at midnight after a bad shift, and I'm totally powerless. I'll watch my own hand reach for another cookie, and another, and I can't stop it. I just can't stop it until the box is empty or my stomach hurts so bad I physically can't swallow another bite. It's terrifying, honestly, to feel like you don't even have a say in what your own hands are doing.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, it's... it's kind of embarrassing, honestly, but I guess that's why I'm here. See, I work these late shifts at the plant, right? So, I'll stop to get something to eat because I haven't had a real break in hours. But it's never just a quick bite. I'll order, like, two double burgers, a large fry, maybe one of those extra-large milkshakes. And I sit in my truck and just... inhale it. \n\nBut that's not even the worst part. You'd think after all that food, I'd be stuffed, right? Ready to just hit the mattress. But I get home, and it's like I haven't eaten a thing all day. I'll go straight out to the extra fridge we keep in the garage—where my wife puts all the leftovers and the bulk groceries—and I just start eating whatever is in there. Cold pasta, half a loaf of bread, whatever. It's like a whole second supper, right on top of the takeout. And I don't even really taste it after a while, I just can't seem to stop myself once I start. It's been happening a lot lately, and it's just... it's really getting out of hand, you know? It's more like... I just feel completely disgusted with myself. You know, I've always thought of myself as a pretty disciplined guy. Dependable, you know? I've been at the plant for years, I work hard, I provide for my family, I show up for my shifts. If there's a job to do, I buckle down and I do it. I'm just not the type to lose control. \n\nBut then... then I'm standing out there in the cold garage at one in the morning, in my socks, scraping the bottom of a Tupperware container, and the reality of what I just did hits me. I feel so ashamed. It's like I'm sneaking around my own house, hiding from my own wife. I'm a 54-year-old man sneaking around like some kid who broke curfew, just so I can stuff my face in the dark. It really makes me feel like I'm letting myself down. I mean, how can a guy who prides himself on being so reliable just throw all his self-control out the window the second he gets home? I just beat myself up over it the whole next day, going over and over it in my head, trying to figure out why I'm so weak when it comes to this... it just really eats at a guy, you know? I'll pull out, say, a family-size bag of chips, or maybe a box of those frosted cookies my wife buys for when the grandkids come over on Sundays. It's the strangest, most frustrating thing. I'll be standing there, chewing, and my own mind is practically screaming at me. I'm literally telling myself, plain as day, \"Roy, shut the bag and go inside. Just roll the top down, put the plastic clip back on, turn off the garage light, and go to bed.\" I'm having this whole rational conversation with myself in my head. \n\nBut it's like my body isn't even listening to me anymore. My hands just keep reaching for more. Hand to bag, hand to mouth, over and over. I'll literally watch my own hand go back into the bag, pull out another handful, and I just keep eating. Even while I'm standing there thinking about how much I'm going to regret it when my alarm goes off at 5 AM for my next shift, I just can't make my hand stop. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body. I used to hear people say things like that and think, well, that's just an excuse for having no willpower. But I'm telling you, I am right there, fully aware, begging myself to just shut the door and walk away, and I just can't do it until the food is completely gone or I feel so stuffed I can't breathe right. It's a terrifying feeling, losing the steering wheel to your own self like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Fast doesn't even... I mean, it's like a race against the clock, doc. When I'm sitting in the cab of my truck after the drive-thru, or later when I'm out in the workshop by that garage fridge, I am just shoveling it in. I eat so fast that, honestly, half the time I barely even taste what I'm eating. \n\nYou'd think if a guy is going to buy all that food, he'd want to sit back and enjoy it, right? Savor it a little bit after a twelve-hour shift on the line. But no, I'm just tearing into the wrappers and practically swallowing it whole. It's this... this frantic energy. I'll be out there in the workshop with whatever I grabbed, and I'm just cramming it down my throat like I’m trying to get it all down before somebody catches me. \n\nWhich is crazy when you think about it, because who's going to catch me? My wife is a heavy sleeper, she's been in bed for hours. But my heart is pounding, and I'm looking over my shoulder at the door to the house, just eating as fast as humanly possible. It's almost like I'm a stray dog that found a scrap in the alley and has to gulp it down before a bigger dog comes along and snatches it away. I don't even chew properly, I just wash it down and keep going. It's this incredible rush to get it over with and hide the evidence, even while I'm still in the middle of doing it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit down and look at it... for the past few months, it’s been happening a lot more than I’d like to admit. I’d say there’s usually at least one night a week, guaranteed, where I end up doing this. But if I'm being completely honest with myself—and with you—it’s often two or three nights a week. \n\nIt almost always hits me after a late shift, or, you know, a really rough day on the line. The schedule at the plant has been absolutely brutal lately, doc. They’ve got us doing these mandatory overtime blocks, and when the machinery acts up—like last week, the main conveyor belt on line three kept seizing up—the foreman just gets so wound up, and that tension just trickles right down to all of us on the floor. You're standing there on the concrete for ten, twelve hours, just trying to keep the quota up. \n\nBy the time I finally punch out, my back is aching, my shoulders are practically up to my ears, and my brain is just totally fried. I'll get in my truck and I try to tell myself, 'Roy, just drive straight home, take a hot shower, and get right into bed. You need the sleep.' But then the stress of the day just sort of... settles into my bones on the drive back, you know? And before I know it, I'm pulling into the driveway, but I'm not going inside to my wife and my bed. I'm heading straight for the workshop and that garage fridge. \n\nSo yeah, it's been a steady two or three times a week for a few months now, just depending on how bad the shifts are. You tell yourself it's just a one-time thing to blow off steam because work was a nightmare, but then Tuesday becomes Thursday, and Thursday becomes Friday, and suddenly the weeks just start piling up and you realize it's become a whole routine.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or upset after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Oh, doc, guilty doesn't even begin to cover it. It's... it's this heavy, sinking feeling that just drops right into the pit of my stomach the second I take that last bite, or the second I finally force myself to stop. I'll be standing there in the cold garage, looking down at the empty chip bags, or the crumbs from those frosted cookies scattered all over the workbench, and this wave of... just absolute disgust washes over me. I feel so ashamed, I really do. \n\nYou see, I've worked at the plant for over twenty years now, right? Started on the floor right out of community college when I realized a desk job wasn't for me. And in all those years, I've built a reputation. Ask anyone on line three, ask the foreman—they'll tell you Roy is the guy you can count on. I show up early, I stay late, I don't complain, I get the job done. I've always prided myself on being disciplined. Being a dependable man, a provider for my wife, someone my grandkids can look up to. That's who I am. Or at least, that's who I thought I was.\n\nBut then I look at what I've just done... sneaking around in my own garage in the middle of the night, cramming food into my mouth like I'm out of my mind, hiding the wrappers at the bottom of the trash can so my wife doesn't see them when she takes the bags out in the morning. It makes me feel like a fraud. Like I'm living this double life. Here I am, this dependable guy during the day, but at night I'm sneaking around like a teenager trying not to get caught breaking curfew. I'm letting myself down, doc. Every single time I do it, I tell myself, 'Never again, Roy, that was the last time.' And then a few days later, I'm right back out there, doing it again, and the shame just piles up higher and higher until I feel like I can barely look at myself in the mirror when I'm shaving the next morning. It's just... it's a terrible way to live with yourself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Control? No. No, not at all. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean... you hit the nail right on the head there, doc. It is the stress. It is absolutely the stress from the plant. Like I was saying before, it is not just that the work is hard. I have done hard work my whole life, you know? Growing up, my old man had me out in the yard doing heavy lifting, fixing up old engines, so I know what physical labor is. I am not afraid of sweating. But this... this is different. It is this constant, grinding pressure. You are standing there on the line, and the conveyor is moving, and you know if you miss one piece, or if the machine calibration is off by even a fraction, the whole batch is ruined. And the foreman, he is always pacing up and down the catwalk, looking at his clipboard, checking his watch. You can practically feel his eyes burning a hole right in the back of your neck. 

So yeah, it builds up this... this tight knot in my chest. I guess you could call it anxiety. I never really thought of myself as an anxious guy, I always thought of anxiety as something people get when they have to give a speech or something, but when I am driving home after a shift like that, my knuckles are completely white on the steering wheel. I grip it so hard my hands actually cramp up. I try turning on the radio, maybe listening to the sports talk station just to drown it all out, but my brain is still back at the factory, just replaying every single little thing that went wrong that day. Did I secure that pressure valve right? Is the morning crew going to complain about the scrap pile? It just spins and spins. 

And by the time I pull into my driveway, I am just so wound up that the idea of going inside and lying down in a quiet, dark bedroom... it just feels impossible. My mind is racing too fast for sleep. I need something to... to push it all down, I guess. To just numb it out and make the spinning stop. So I go straight to the garage. Eating all that junk, it is like it is the only thing that gives me a minute of quiet in my head, even if it makes me feel like absolute garbage afterward. 

So, yeah, if you are asking if work is the trigger... I really do not know what else it could be. Because on my days off, if I am just puttering around the yard or watching the grandkids, I do not even look twice at the garage fridge. I just eat a normal dinner with my wife and that is that. It is always, always after those brutal shifts when my brain just will not shut off.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, it's... it's kind of embarrassing, honestly, but I guess that's why I'm here. See, I work these late shifts at the plant, right? And by the time I clock out, it's dark, I'm exhausted, my feet are killing me, and the only thing open is the drive-thru. So, I'll stop to get something to eat because I haven't had a real break in hours. But it's never just a quick bite. I'll order, like, two double burgers, a large fry, maybe one of those extra-large milkshakes. And I sit in my truck and just... inhale it. \n\nBut that's not even the worst part. You'd think after all that food, I'd be stuffed, right? Ready to just hit the mattress. But I get home, and it's like I haven't eaten a thing all day. I'll go straight out to the extra fridge we keep in the garage—where my wife puts all the leftovers and the bulk groceries—and I just start eating whatever is in there. Cold pasta, half a loaf of bread, whatever. It's like a whole second supper, right on top of the takeout. And I don't even really taste it after a while, I just can't seem to stop myself once I start. It's been happening a lot lately, and it's just... it's really getting out of hand, you know? I just don't know why I keep doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah... guilty doesn't even really begin to cover it, doc. It's more like... I just feel completely disgusted with myself. You know, I've always thought of myself as a pretty disciplined guy. Dependable, you know? I've been at the plant for years, I work hard, I provide for my family, I show up for my shifts. If there's a job to do, I buckle down and I do it. I'm just not the type to lose control. \n\nBut then... then I'm standing out there in the cold garage at one in the morning, in my socks, scraping the bottom of a Tupperware container, and the reality of what I just did hits me. I feel so ashamed. It's like I'm sneaking around my own house, hiding from my own wife. I'm a 54-year-old man sneaking around like some kid who broke curfew, just so I can stuff my face in the dark. It really makes me feel like I'm letting myself down. I mean, how can a guy who prides himself on being so reliable just throw all his self-control out the window the second he gets home? I just beat myself up over it the whole next day, going over and over it in my head, trying to figure out why I'm so weak when it comes to this... it just really eats at a guy, you know? Feeling like you can't even trust yourself in your own kitchen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you start during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: That is exactly what it is, doc. That might actually be the scariest part of this whole thing for me. I'll be standing out there—like I said, by that old Kenmore fridge we keep in the garage, the one that hums real loud—and the cold air is just hitting my chest. I'll pull out, say, a family-size bag of chips, or maybe a box of those frosted cookies my wife buys for when the grandkids come over on Sundays. And I'll take that first bite, thinking, you know, just a taste to settle my stomach after the burgers. \n\nBut once I start... it feels like a switch is flipped in my brain. It's the strangest, most frustrating thing. I'll be standing there, chewing, and my own mind is practically screaming at me. I'm literally telling myself, plain as day, \"Roy, shut the bag and go inside. Just roll the top down, put the plastic clip back on, turn off the garage light, and go to bed.\" I'm having this whole rational conversation with myself in my head. \n\nBut it's like my body isn't even listening to me anymore. My hands just keep reaching for more. Hand to bag, hand to mouth, over and over. I'll literally watch my own hand go back into the bag, pull out another handful, and I just keep eating. Even while I'm standing there thinking about how much I'm going to regret it when my alarm goes off at 5 AM for my next shift, I just can't make my hand stop. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body. I used to hear people say things like that and think, well, that's just an excuse for having no willpower. But I'm telling you, I am right there, fully aware, begging myself to just shut the door and walk away, and I just can't do it until the food is completely gone or I feel so stuffed I can't breathe right. It's a terrifying feeling, losing the steering wheel to your own self like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Fast doesn't even... I mean, it's like a race against the clock, doc. When I'm sitting in the cab of my truck after the drive-thru, or later when I'm out in the workshop by that garage fridge, I am just shoveling it in. I eat so fast that, honestly, half the time I barely even taste what I'm eating. \n\nYou'd think if a guy is going to buy all that food, he'd want to sit back and enjoy it, right? Savor it a little bit after a twelve-hour shift on the line. But no, I'm just tearing into the wrappers and practically swallowing it whole. It's this... this frantic energy. I'll be out there in the workshop with whatever I grabbed, and I'm just cramming it down my throat like I’m trying to get it all down before somebody catches me. \n\nWhich is crazy when you think about it, because who's going to catch me? My wife is a heavy sleeper, she's been in bed for hours. But my heart is pounding, and I'm looking over my shoulder at the door to the house, just eating as fast as humanly possible. It's almost like I'm a stray dog that found a scrap in the alley and has to gulp it down before a bigger dog comes along and snatches it away. I don't even chew properly, I just wash it down and keep going. It's this incredible rush to get it over with and hide the evidence, even while I'm still in the middle of doing it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit down and look at it... for the past few months, it’s been happening a lot more than I’d like to admit. I’d say there’s usually at least one night a week, guaranteed, where I end up doing this. But if I'm being completely honest with myself—and with you—it’s often two or three nights a week. \n\nIt almost always hits me after a late shift, or, you know, a really rough day on the line. The schedule at the plant has been absolutely brutal lately, doc. They’ve got us doing these mandatory overtime blocks, and when the machinery acts up—like last week, the main conveyor belt on line three kept seizing up—the foreman just gets so wound up, and that tension just trickles right down to all of us on the floor. You're standing there on the concrete for ten, twelve hours, just trying to keep the quota up. \n\nBy the time I finally punch out, my back is aching, my shoulders are practically up to my ears, and my brain is just totally fried. I'll get in my truck and I try to tell myself, 'Roy, just drive straight home, take a hot shower, and get right into bed. You need the sleep.' But then the stress of the day just sort of... settles into my bones on the drive back, you know? And before I know it, I'm pulling into the driveway, but I'm not going inside to my wife and my bed. I'm heading straight for the workshop and that garage fridge. \n\nSo yeah, it's been a steady two or three times a week for a few months now, just depending on how bad the shifts are. You tell yourself it's just a one-time thing to blow off steam because work was a nightmare, but then Tuesday becomes Thursday, and Thursday becomes Friday, and suddenly the weeks just start piling up and you realize it's become a whole routine.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or upset after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Oh, doc, guilty doesn't even begin to cover it. It's... it's this heavy, sinking feeling that just drops right into the pit of my stomach the second I take that last bite, or the second I finally force myself to stop. I'll be standing there in the cold garage, looking down at the empty chip bags, or the crumbs from those frosted cookies scattered all over the workbench, and this wave of... just absolute disgust washes over me. I feel so ashamed, I really do. \n\nYou see, I've worked at the plant for over twenty years now, right? Started on the floor right out of community college when I realized a desk job wasn't for me. And in all those years, I've built a reputation. Ask anyone on line three, ask the foreman—they'll tell you Roy is the guy you can count on. I show up early, I stay late, I don't complain, I get the job done. I've always prided myself on being disciplined. Being a dependable man, a provider for my wife, someone my grandkids can look up to. That's who I am. Or at least, that's who I thought I was.\n\nBut then I look at what I've just done... sneaking around in my own garage in the middle of the night, cramming food into my mouth like I'm out of my mind, hiding the wrappers at the bottom of the trash can so my wife doesn't see them when she takes the bags out in the morning. It makes me feel like a fraud. Like I'm living this double life. Here I am, this dependable guy during the day, but at night I'm sneaking around like a teenager trying not to get caught breaking curfew. I'm letting myself down, doc. Every single time I do it, I tell myself, 'Never again, Roy, that was the last time.' And then a few days later, I'm right back out there, doing it again, and the shame just piles up higher and higher until I feel like I can barely look at myself in the mirror when I'm shaving the next morning. It's just... it's a terrible way to live with yourself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Control? No. No, not at all. That's the scariest part of the whole thing, doc. It's like... well, it's exactly like someone else takes the wheel, you know? I'll be out there, standing by the workbench, and maybe I just went out to grab a bottle of water or put away a tool I left out from the weekend. But then I open that fridge, or I see the bag of chips I stashed behind the paint cans, and it's like... click. A switch just flips in my brain. \n\nOnce I start, once I take that first bite, the control is just gone. Completely gone. I'll actually be standing there, chewing, and my own mind is screaming at me. I'm literally telling myself, 'Roy, shut the bag. Just roll the top down, put it away, and go inside. Go to bed.' I hear the voice clear as day. But my hands... my hands just aren't listening to me. They just keep reaching into the bag, pulling out more, shoving it in. \n\nIt's the strangest, most helpless feeling in the world. You're a grown man, you've worked hard your whole life, you pride yourself on discipline, like I said before. I can stand on that factory floor for twelve hours and not take a break until the whistle blows, because I have control over my body and my work. But put me in front of that garage fridge at midnight after a bad shift, and I'm totally powerless. I'll watch my own hand reach for another cookie, and another, and I can't stop it. I just can't stop it until the box is empty or my stomach hurts so bad I physically can't swallow another bite. It's terrifying, honestly, to feel like you don't even have a say in what your own hands are doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's something specific that triggers these late-night eating episodes, like stress or anxiety from your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... you hit the nail right on the head there, doc. It is the stress. It is absolutely the stress from the plant. Like I was saying before, it is not just that the work is hard. I have done hard work my whole life, you know? Growing up, my old man had me out in the yard doing heavy lifting, fixing up old engines, so I know what physical labor is. I am not afraid of sweating. But this... this is different. It is this constant, grinding pressure. You are standing there on the line, and the conveyor is moving, and you know if you miss one piece, or if the machine calibration is off by even a fraction, the whole batch is ruined. And the foreman, he is always pacing up and down the catwalk, looking at his clipboard, checking his watch. You can practically feel his eyes burning a hole right in the back of your neck. \n\nSo yeah, it builds up this... this tight knot in my chest. I guess you could call it anxiety. I never really thought of myself as an anxious guy, I always thought of anxiety as something people get when they have to give a speech or something, but when I am driving home after a shift like that, my knuckles are completely white on the steering wheel. I grip it so hard my hands actually cramp up. I try turning on the radio, maybe listening to the sports talk station just to drown it all out, but my brain is still back at the factory, just replaying every single little thing that went wrong that day. Did I secure that pressure valve right? Is the morning crew going to complain about the scrap pile? It just spins and spins. \n\nAnd by the time I pull into my driveway, I am just so wound up that the idea of going inside and lying down in a quiet, dark bedroom... it just feels impossible. My mind is racing too fast for sleep. I need something to... to push it all down, I guess. To just numb it out and make the spinning stop. So I go straight to the garage. Eating all that junk, it is like it is the only thing that gives me a minute of quiet in my head, even if it makes me feel like absolute garbage afterward. \n\nSo, yeah, if you are asking if work is the trigger... I really do not know what else it could be. Because on my days off, if I am just puttering around the yard or watching the grandkids, I do not even look twice at the garage fridge. I just eat a normal dinner with my wife and that is that. It is always, always after those brutal shifts when my brain just will not shut off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

A switch just flips in my brain. \n\nOnce I start, once I take that first bite, the control is just gone. Completely gone. I'll actually be standing there, chewing, and my own mind is screaming at me. I'm literally telling myself, 'Roy, shut the bag. Just roll the top down, put it away, and go inside. Go to bed.' I hear the voice clear as day. But my hands... my hands just aren't listening to me. They just keep reaching into the bag, pulling out more, shoving it in. \n\nIt's the strangest, most helpless feeling in the world. You're a grown man, you've worked hard your whole life, you pride yourself on discipline, like I said before. I can stand on that factory floor for twelve hours and not take a break until the whistle blows, because I have control over my body and my work. But put me in front of that garage fridge at midnight after a bad shift, and I'm totally powerless. I'll watch my own hand reach for another cookie, and another, and I can't stop it. I just can't stop it until the box is empty or my stomach hurts so bad I physically can't swallow another bite. It's terrifying, honestly, to feel like you don't even have a say in what your own hands are doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's something specific that triggers these late-night eating episodes, like stress or anxiety from your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... you hit the nail right on the head there, doc. It is the stress. It is absolutely the stress from the plant. Like I was saying before, it is not just that the work is hard. I have done hard work my whole life, you know? Growing up, my old man had me out in the yard doing heavy lifting, fixing up old engines, so I know what physical labor is. I am not afraid of sweating. But this... this is different. It is this constant, grinding pressure. You are standing there on the line, and the conveyor is moving, and you know if you miss one piece, or if the machine calibration is off by even a fraction, the whole batch is ruined. And the foreman, he is always pacing up and down the catwalk, looking at his clipboard, checking his watch. You can practically feel his eyes burning a hole right in the back of your neck. \n\nSo yeah, it builds up this... this tight knot in my chest. I guess you could call it anxiety. I never really thought of myself as an anxious guy, I always thought of anxiety as something people get when they have to give a speech or something, but when I am driving home after a shift like that, my knuckles are completely white on the steering wheel. I grip it so hard my hands actually cramp up. I try turning on the radio, maybe listening to the sports talk station just to drown it all out, but my brain is still back at the factory, just replaying every single little thing that went wrong that day. Did I secure that pressure valve right? Is the morning crew going to complain about the scrap pile? It just spins and spins. \n\nAnd by the time I pull into my driveway, I am just so wound up that the idea of going inside and lying down in a quiet, dark bedroom... it just feels impossible. My mind is racing too fast for sleep. I need something to... to push it all down, I guess. To just numb it out and make the spinning stop. So I go straight to the garage. Eating all that junk, it is like it is the only thing that gives me a minute of quiet in my head, even if it makes me feel like absolute garbage afterward. \n\nSo, yeah, if you are asking if work is the trigger... I really do not know what else it could be. Because on my days off, if I am just puttering around the yard or watching the grandkids, I do not even look twice at the garage fridge. I just eat a normal dinner with my wife and that is that. It is always, always after those brutal shifts when my brain just will not shut off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, absolutely. Uncomfortable doesn't even begin to cover the half of it, doc. You know, when I'm in the middle of it, like I was saying before about that switch flipping in my brain, I don't really feel anything at all. It's like my stomach is just this bottomless pit while I'm standing there at the workbench shoving it all in. I don't register the fullness while it's happening. But then... then I finally stop. 

And by the time I actually do stop, my stomach is just... it's so incredibly tight against my work pants. I wear these heavy-duty canvas trousers to the plant, you know, the kind with the reinforced knees and a real stiff waistband because you need something durable when you're rubbing up against the machinery all day. Well, after one of these episodes, that waistband is digging into my gut so hard it actually leaves a deep red mark on my skin. I'll end up just sitting there on this old metal stool I keep by the tool chest, leaned all the way back against the drywall, just completely stuffed. 

It gets so bad, I am uncomfortable enough that I don't want to move for a while. I literally can't. I just sit there, staring at the oil stains on the concrete floor, feeling like I swallowed a bowling ball. I can't bend over to pick up the wrappers I dropped, I can't stand up to reach the pull-string for the overhead fluorescent light... it takes a good twenty, maybe thirty minutes before I can even muster the energy or the physical ability to heave myself off that stool and make my way into the house. And then trying to sleep like that? Lying flat on my back in bed while my stomach feels like it's stretching right out of my skin, listening to my wife breathe and hoping I don't wake her up with how much I'm shifting around trying to find a position that doesn't physically hurt... it's just miserable. You'd think a rational man would remember that exact feeling of his pants cutting off his circulation and use it to stop himself the next time, right? You'd think I'd remember sitting on that stool in pain, but when that stress hits after a shift, it's like all that memory just gets wiped clean.

It really makes me feel like I'm letting myself down. I mean, how can a guy who prides himself on being so reliable just throw all his self-control out the window the second he gets home? That might actually be the scariest part of this whole thing for me. I'll be standing out there—like I said, by that old Kenmore fridge we keep in the garage, the one that hums real loud—and the cold air is just hitting my chest. I'll pull out, say, a family-size bag of chips, or maybe a box of those frosted cookies my wife buys for when the grandkids come over on Sundays. And I'll take that first bite, thinking, you know, just a taste to settle my stomach after the burgers. \n\nBut once I start... it feels like a switch is flipped in my brain. It's the strangest, most frustrating thing. I'll be standing there, chewing, and my own mind is practically screaming at me. I'm literally telling myself, plain as day, \"Roy, shut the bag and go inside. Just roll the top down, put the plastic clip back on, turn off the garage light, and go to bed.\" I'm having this whole rational conversation with myself in my head. \n\nBut it's like my body isn't even listening to me anymore. My hands just keep reaching for more. Hand to bag, hand to mouth, over and over. I'll literally watch my own hand go back into the bag, pull out another handful, and I just keep eating. Even while I'm standing there thinking about how much I'm going to regret it when my alarm goes off at 5 AM for my next shift, I just can't make my hand stop. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body. I used to hear people say things like that and think, well, that's just an excuse for having no willpower. But I'm telling you, I am right there, fully aware, begging myself to just shut the door and walk away, and I just can't do it until the food is completely gone or I feel so stuffed I can't breathe right. It's a terrifying feeling, losing the steering wheel to your own self like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Fast doesn't even... I mean, it's like a race against the clock, doc. When I'm sitting in the cab of my truck after the drive-thru, or later when I'm out in the workshop by that garage fridge, I am just shoveling it in. I eat so fast that, honestly, half the time I barely even taste what I'm eating. \n\nYou'd think if a guy is going to buy all that food, he'd want to sit back and enjoy it, right? Savor it a little bit after a twelve-hour shift on the line. But no, I'm just tearing into the wrappers and practically swallowing it whole. It's this... this frantic energy. I'll be out there in the workshop with whatever I grabbed, and I'm just cramming it down my throat like I’m trying to get it all down before somebody catches me. \n\nWhich is crazy when you think about it, because who's going to catch me? My wife is a heavy sleeper, she's been in bed for hours. But my heart is pounding, and I'm looking over my shoulder at the door to the house, just eating as fast as humanly possible. It's almost like I'm a stray dog that found a scrap in the alley and has to gulp it down before a bigger dog comes along and snatches it away. I don't even chew properly, I just wash it down and keep going. It's this incredible rush to get it over with and hide the evidence, even while I'm still in the middle of doing it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit down and look at it... for the past few months, it’s been happening a lot more than I’d like to admit. I’d say there’s usually at least one night a week, guaranteed, where I end up doing this. But if I'm being completely honest with myself—and with you—it’s often two or three nights a week. \n\nIt almost always hits me after a late shift, or, you know, a really rough day on the line. The schedule at the plant has been absolutely brutal lately, doc. They’ve got us doing these mandatory overtime blocks, and when the machinery acts up—like last week, the main conveyor belt on line three kept seizing up—the foreman just gets so wound up, and that tension just trickles right down to all of us on the floor. You're standing there on the concrete for ten, twelve hours, just trying to keep the quota up. \n\nBy the time I finally punch out, my back is aching, my shoulders are practically up to my ears, and my brain is just totally fried. I'll get in my truck and I try to tell myself, 'Roy, just drive straight home, take a hot shower, and get right into bed. You need the sleep.' But then the stress of the day just sort of... settles into my bones on the drive back, you know? And before I know it, I'm pulling into the driveway, but I'm not going inside to my wife and my bed. I'm heading straight for the workshop and that garage fridge. \n\nSo yeah, it's been a steady two or three times a week for a few months now, just depending on how bad the shifts are. You tell yourself it's just a one-time thing to blow off steam because work was a nightmare, but then Tuesday becomes Thursday, and Thursday becomes Friday, and suddenly the weeks just start piling up and you realize it's become a whole routine.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or upset after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Oh, doc, guilty doesn't even begin to cover it. It's... it's this heavy, sinking feeling that just drops right into the pit of my stomach the second I take that last bite, or the second I finally force myself to stop. I'll be standing there in the cold garage, looking down at the empty chip bags, or the crumbs from those frosted cookies scattered all over the workbench, and this wave of... just absolute disgust washes over me. I feel so ashamed, I really do. \n\nYou see, I've worked at the plant for over twenty years now, right? Started on the floor right out of community college when I realized a desk job wasn't for me. And in all those years, I've built a reputation. Ask anyone on line three, ask the foreman—they'll tell you Roy is the guy you can count on. I show up early, I stay late, I don't complain, I get the job done. I've always prided myself on being disciplined. Being a dependable man, a provider for my wife, someone my grandkids can look up to. That's who I am. Or at least, that's who I thought I was.\n\nBut then I look at what I've just done... sneaking around in my own garage in the middle of the night, cramming food into my mouth like I'm out of my mind, hiding the wrappers at the bottom of the trash can so my wife doesn't see them when she takes the bags out in the morning. It makes me feel like a fraud. Like I'm living this double life. Here I am, this dependable guy during the day, but at night I'm sneaking around like a teenager trying not to get caught breaking curfew. I'm letting myself down, doc. Every single time I do it, I tell myself, 'Never again, Roy, that was the last time.' And then a few days later, I'm right back out there, doing it again, and the shame just piles up higher and higher until I feel like I can barely look at myself in the mirror when I'm shaving the next morning. It's just... it's a terrible way to live with yourself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Control? No. No, not at all. That's the scariest part of the whole thing, doc. It's like... well, it's exactly like someone else takes the wheel, you know? I'll be out there, standing by the workbench, and maybe I just went out to grab a bottle of water or put away a tool I left out from the weekend. But then I open that fridge, or I see the bag of chips I stashed behind the paint cans, and it's like... click. A switch just flips in my brain. \n\nOnce I start, once I take that first bite, the control is just gone. Completely gone. I'll actually be standing there, chewing, and my own mind is screaming at me. I'm literally telling myself, 'Roy, shut the bag. Just roll the top down, put it away, and go inside. Go to bed.' I hear the voice clear as day. But my hands... my hands just aren't listening to me. They just keep reaching into the bag, pulling out more, shoving it in. \n\nIt's the strangest, most helpless feeling in the world. You're a grown man, you've worked hard your whole life, you pride yourself on discipline, like I said before. I can stand on that factory floor for twelve hours and not take a break until the whistle blows, because I have control over my body and my work. But put me in front of that garage fridge at midnight after a bad shift, and I'm totally powerless. I'll watch my own hand reach for another cookie, and another, and I can't stop it. I just can't stop it until the box is empty or my stomach hurts so bad I physically can't swallow another bite. It's terrifying, honestly, to feel like you don't even have a say in what your own hands are doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's something specific that triggers these late-night eating episodes, like stress or anxiety from your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... you hit the nail right on the head there, doc. It is the stress. It is absolutely the stress from the plant. Like I was saying before, it is not just that the work is hard. I have done hard work my whole life, you know? Growing up, my old man had me out in the yard doing heavy lifting, fixing up old engines, so I know what physical labor is. I am not afraid of sweating. But this... this is different. It is this constant, grinding pressure. You are standing there on the line, and the conveyor is moving, and you know if you miss one piece, or if the machine calibration is off by even a fraction, the whole batch is ruined. And the foreman, he is always pacing up and down the catwalk, looking at his clipboard, checking his watch. You can practically feel his eyes burning a hole right in the back of your neck. \n\nSo yeah, it builds up this... this tight knot in my chest. I guess you could call it anxiety. I never really thought of myself as an anxious guy, I always thought of anxiety as something people get when they have to give a speech or something, but when I am driving home after a shift like that, my knuckles are completely white on the steering wheel. I grip it so hard my hands actually cramp up. I try turning on the radio, maybe listening to the sports talk station just to drown it all out, but my brain is still back at the factory, just replaying every single little thing that went wrong that day. Did I secure that pressure valve right? Is the morning crew going to complain about the scrap pile? It just spins and spins. \n\nAnd by the time I pull into my driveway, I am just so wound up that the idea of going inside and lying down in a quiet, dark bedroom... it just feels impossible. My mind is racing too fast for sleep. I need something to... to push it all down, I guess. To just numb it out and make the spinning stop. So I go straight to the garage. Eating all that junk, it is like it is the only thing that gives me a minute of quiet in my head, even if it makes me feel like absolute garbage afterward. \n\nSo, yeah, if you are asking if work is the trigger... I really do not know what else it could be. Because on my days off, if I am just puttering around the yard or watching the grandkids, I do not even look twice at the garage fridge. I just eat a normal dinner with my wife and that is that. It is always, always after those brutal shifts when my brain just will not shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel uncomfortably full after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Uncomfortable doesn't even begin to cover the half of it, doc. You know, when I'm in the middle of it, like I was saying before about that switch flipping in my brain, I don't really feel anything at all. It's like my stomach is just this bottomless pit while I'm standing there at the workbench shoving it all in. I don't register the fullness while it's happening. But then... then I finally stop. \n\nAnd by the time I actually do stop, my stomach is just... it's so incredibly tight against my work pants. I wear these heavy-duty canvas trousers to the plant, you know, the kind with the reinforced knees and a real stiff waistband because you need something durable when you're rubbing up against the machinery all day. Well, after one of these episodes, that waistband is digging into my gut so hard it actually leaves a deep red mark on my skin. I'll end up just sitting there on this old metal stool I keep by the tool chest, leaned all the way back against the drywall, just completely stuffed. \n\nIt gets so bad, I am uncomfortable enough that I don't want to move for a while. I literally can't. I just sit there, staring at the oil stains on the concrete floor, feeling like I swallowed a bowling ball. I can't bend over to pick up the wrappers I dropped, I can't stand up to reach the pull-string for the overhead fluorescent light... it takes a good twenty, maybe thirty minutes before I can even muster the energy or the physical ability to heave myself off that stool and make my way into the house. And then trying to sleep like that? Lying flat on my back in bed while my stomach feels like it's stretching right out of my skin, listening to my wife breathe and hoping I don't wake her up with how much I'm shifting around trying to find a position that doesn't physically hurt... it's just miserable. You'd think a rational man would remember that exact feeling of his pants cutting off his circulation and use it to stop himself the next time, right? You'd think I'd remember sitting on that stool in pain, but when that stress hits after a shift, it's like all that memory just gets wiped clean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, doc, exactly. Like I was trying to explain earlier, that is the exact feeling, and it's the part that drives me the craziest. It's just like I said about that switch flipping in my head—once that happens, what I actually *want* to do doesn't matter a lick. 

I can be standing there by the workbench, completely aware of what's happening, and my rational brain is fighting it the whole time. I'll be staring at the chips or maybe a container of leftover casserole I found in the back of the fridge, and I'm telling myself, over and over, 'Roy, just put the lid back on. Shut the bag, turn off the garage light, and just go inside to your wife.' I want to stop. I really, truly want to stop, especially knowing how bad my stomach is going to hurt later. But my hands... they just don't care what my brain wants. 

They just keep reaching out, grabbing more, and bringing it to my mouth. It's like they're on some kind of automatic factory setting that I can't override, like a machine on the assembly line that just keeps punching out parts even when the conveyor belt is jammed. I can literally watch my own arm reaching back into the box for another handful, and I'm pleading with myself to just walk away, but I just cannot make the physical motion stop. My hands just keep going back for more, over and over, until the food is completely gone. It's the most frustrating thing in the world, being trapped in your own body like that, watching yourself do something you are actively trying to talk yourself out of.
