I really do not know what else it could be. Because on my days off, if I am just puttering around the yard or watching the grandkids, I do not even look twice at the garage fridge. I just eat a normal dinner with my wife and that is that. It is always, always after those brutal shifts when my brain just will not shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel uncomfortably full after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Uncomfortable doesn't even begin to cover the half of it, doc. You know, when I'm in the middle of it, like I was saying before about that switch flipping in my brain, I don't really feel anything at all. It's like my stomach is just this bottomless pit while I'm standing there at the workbench shoving it all in. I don't register the fullness while it's happening. But then... then I finally stop. \n\nAnd by the time I actually do stop, my stomach is just... it's so incredibly tight against my work pants. I wear these heavy-duty canvas trousers to the plant, you know, the kind with the reinforced knees and a real stiff waistband because you need something durable when you're rubbing up against the machinery all day. Well, after one of these episodes, that waistband is digging into my gut so hard it actually leaves a deep red mark on my skin. I'll end up just sitting there on this old metal stool I keep by the tool chest, leaned all the way back against the drywall, just completely stuffed. \n\nIt gets so bad, I am uncomfortable enough that I don't want to move for a while. I literally can't. I just sit there, staring at the oil stains on the concrete floor, feeling like I swallowed a bowling ball. I can't bend over to pick up the wrappers I dropped, I can't stand up to reach the pull-string for the overhead fluorescent light... it takes a good twenty, maybe thirty minutes before I can even muster the energy or the physical ability to heave myself off that stool and make my way into the house. And then trying to sleep like that? Lying flat on my back in bed while my stomach feels like it's stretching right out of my skin, listening to my wife breathe and hoping I don't wake her up with how much I'm shifting around trying to find a position that doesn't physically hurt... it's just miserable. You'd think a rational man would remember that exact feeling of his pants cutting off his circulation and use it to stop himself the next time, right? You'd think I'd remember sitting on that stool in pain, but when that stress hits after a shift, it's like all that memory just gets wiped clean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what or how much you're eating during these episodes even when you want to stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, doc, exactly. Like I was trying to explain earlier, that is the exact feeling, and it's the part that drives me the craziest. It's just like I said about that switch flipping in my head—once that happens, what I actually *want* to do doesn't matter a lick. \n\nI can be standing there by the workbench, completely aware of what's happening, and my rational brain is fighting it the whole time. I'll be staring at the chips or maybe a container of leftover casserole I found in the back of the fridge, and I'm telling myself, over and over, 'Roy, just put the lid back on. Shut the bag, turn off the garage light, and just go inside to your wife.' I want to stop. I really, truly want to stop, especially knowing how bad my stomach is going to hurt later. But my hands... they just don't care what my brain wants. \n\nThey just keep reaching out, grabbing more, and bringing it to my mouth. It's like they're on some kind of automatic factory setting that I can't override, like a machine on the assembly line that just keeps punching out parts even when the conveyor belt is jammed. I can literally watch my own arm reaching back into the box for another handful, and I'm pleading with myself to just walk away, but I just cannot make the physical motion stop. My hands just keep going back for more, over and over, until the food is completely gone. It's the most frustrating thing in the world, being trapped in your own body like that, watching yourself do something you are actively trying to talk yourself out of.\n\nDoctor: Has your late-night eating affected your relationships with your family or your performance at work?\n\nPatient: Well, as far as the plant goes, no, I wouldn't say it's affecting my performance. I'm still getting to work on time every day, punching the clock, doing what needs to be done. I haven't missed a single shift, and my quotas are exactly where they need to be. And outside of work, you know, I'm still keeping up with church. My wife and I, we're in our same pew every Sunday morning, haven't missed a service. Pastor Jim would probably send a search party if we did, seeing as we've been going there for twenty-odd years. So, the outside world... I think I'm holding that together just fine. People looking at me probably think everything is completely normal.\n\nBut at home... yeah. It's definitely affecting things there. Not that we're yelling or fighting or anything like that, but... I've just been a lot more withdrawn. It’s just... well, it takes so much energy, doc. So much mental energy just trying to cover my tracks. \n\nLike I was saying about coming home from those shifts—if I get off a day shift and I stop at the convenience store on the way back, or I go straight into the garage and eat all that junk before I go inside... by the time I finally walk through the front door into the kitchen, my wife is usually just finishing up making dinner. And she’s a wonderful cook, she really is. She’ll have a nice pot roast or a meatloaf going, she's setting the table, asking me how my day was... and I’m standing there with my stomach already stretched to the bursting point like I described earlier. \n\nSo I have to stand there and lie to her. I have to make up all these excuses about why I’m not hungry for dinner. I’ll tell her, \"Oh, the guys brought in pizzas for a retirement thing late in the afternoon,\" or \"My stomach is just a little sour from the fumes at the plant today.\" And then I have to make sure I sneak out later and bury the wrappers deep in the outside trash bin so she doesn't find them when she takes the kitchen bag out. It's just exhausting. I spend all this energy keeping the secret, covering up where I stopped and what I did, that by the time I sit down in my recliner in the living room, I don't even want to talk. I just stare at the TV and give her one-word answers. I'm just so wrapped up in hiding it all that I'm completely pulling away from her in our own house.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant weight gain since these late-night eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, doc, because you would really think so, right? With the sheer amount of food I'm packing away out there by the workbench, you'd expect me to be buying new pants every other month. But honestly... no. I really haven't noticed any significant weight gain at all. \n\nI mean, I step on that old dial scale we've had in our master bathroom since we bought the place—the one with the little rubber mat that's starting to peel up at the corners—and the needle pretty much hovers right exactly where it always has for the last, oh, ten or fifteen years. I've always been a sturdy guy, you know, broad-shouldered, carrying a little extra around the middle, but my weight hasn't suddenly spiked or ballooned out of control since these late-night episodes started happening. \n\nI figure a lot of that has to do with the plant. People who work desk jobs don't always realize how physical production work actually is. Even when you're just monitoring the line, you are on your feet on that hard, unforgiving concrete for eight, sometimes ten or twelve hours if we're pulling overtime to meet a quota. You're constantly walking the floor, climbing up and down those steep metal catwalks to check the hoppers, hauling materials around... and just sweating buckets in the summer because the ventilation in that building is an absolute joke. So I guess I'm just burning a lot of those calories off just doing my day-to-day job. \n\nLike I was saying about those heavy canvas work trousers earlier—sure, they cut into my gut like a wire right after I finish eating all that junk in the garage, but by the time my alarm goes off at 4:30 the next morning and I'm getting dressed in the dark so I don't wake my wife, I button them up and they fit exactly the same as they always do. I'm still using the exact same notch on my leather belt that I've used for years. So, no, no real weight gain to speak of. Which I guess is a blessing, but it almost makes it more confusing, you know? Like my body is just hiding all the evidence of what I'm doing out there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, doc, that's... that's probably the hardest part to talk about, honestly. It really is. Because once that switch flips back, you know? Like I was saying earlier about my hands just moving on their own like an assembly line—well, eventually the line stops. The food is gone. And then I'm just standing there in the garage, usually under that one flickering fluorescent bulb over the workbench that I've been meaning to replace for six months, just staring down at a pile of empty wrappers or a scraped-clean Tupperware container. 

And the feeling that washes over me right then... it's just pure shame. I feel so ashamed, and honestly, just completely disgusted with myself. 

You have to understand, I’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined guy. I’ve worked at the plant for decades, I provide for my wife, I show up to church every Sunday, I pay the mortgage on time. People rely on me. I’m dependable. If someone needs a shift covered, they call me. If a machine breaks down and needs a steady hand, they call me. I've built my whole life on being the guy who holds it together, the guy who does what he's supposed to do, when he's supposed to do it. I've never been one to lose control.

So to be standing out there in the dark, wiping grease off my chin and hiding evidence from my own wife... it makes me feel like I’m just sneaking around like some kind of thief in my own home. It’s pathetic, is what it is. I feel like I'm completely letting myself down. I just stand there looking at the mess and think, 'What kind of a man does this at your age?' It completely shatters how I see myself. I mean, how can I be this dependable, rock-solid guy out in the world, but I can't even walk past a box of crackers in my own garage without losing my mind? It just eats away at me. I'll just sit out there on my work stool sometimes afterward, before I finally go inside to face my wife, just feeling sick to my stomach—and not just from the sheer amount of food, but from the disgust. It just makes me feel so small.

It really makes me feel like I'm letting myself down. I mean, how can a guy who prides himself on being so reliable just throw all his self-control out the window the second he gets home? I just beat myself up over it the whole next day, going over and over it in my head, trying to figure out why I'm so weak when it comes to this... it just really eats at a guy, you know? Feeling like you can't even trust yourself in your own kitchen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you start during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: That is exactly what it is, doc. That might actually be the scariest part of this whole thing for me. I'll be standing out there—like I said, by that old Kenmore fridge we keep in the garage, the one that hums real loud—and the cold air is just hitting my chest. I'll pull out, say, a family-size bag of chips, or maybe a box of those frosted cookies my wife buys for when the grandkids come over on Sundays. And I'll take that first bite, thinking, you know, just a taste to settle my stomach after the burgers. \n\nBut once I start... it feels like a switch is flipped in my brain. It's the strangest, most frustrating thing. I'll be standing there, chewing, and my own mind is practically screaming at me. I'm literally telling myself, plain as day, \"Roy, shut the bag and go inside. Just roll the top down, put the plastic clip back on, turn off the garage light, and go to bed.\" I'm having this whole rational conversation with myself in my head. \n\nBut it's like my body isn't even listening to me anymore. My hands just keep reaching for more. Hand to bag, hand to mouth, over and over. I'll literally watch my own hand go back into the bag, pull out another handful, and I just keep eating. Even while I'm standing there thinking about how much I'm going to regret it when my alarm goes off at 5 AM for my next shift, I just can't make my hand stop. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body. I used to hear people say things like that and think, well, that's just an excuse for having no willpower. But I'm telling you, I am right there, fully aware, begging myself to just shut the door and walk away, and I just can't do it until the food is completely gone or I feel so stuffed I can't breathe right. It's a terrifying feeling, losing the steering wheel to your own self like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Fast doesn't even... I mean, it's like a race against the clock, doc. When I'm sitting in the cab of my truck after the drive-thru, or later when I'm out in the workshop by that garage fridge, I am just shoveling it in. I eat so fast that, honestly, half the time I barely even taste what I'm eating. \n\nYou'd think if a guy is going to buy all that food, he'd want to sit back and enjoy it, right? Savor it a little bit after a twelve-hour shift on the line. But no, I'm just tearing into the wrappers and practically swallowing it whole. It's this... this frantic energy. I'll be out there in the workshop with whatever I grabbed, and I'm just cramming it down my throat like I’m trying to get it all down before somebody catches me. \n\nWhich is crazy when you think about it, because who's going to catch me? My wife is a heavy sleeper, she's been in bed for hours. But my heart is pounding, and I'm looking over my shoulder at the door to the house, just eating as fast as humanly possible. It's almost like I'm a stray dog that found a scrap in the alley and has to gulp it down before a bigger dog comes along and snatches it away. I don't even chew properly, I just wash it down and keep going. It's this incredible rush to get it over with and hide the evidence, even while I'm still in the middle of doing it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit down and look at it... for the past few months, it’s been happening a lot more than I’d like to admit. I’d say there’s usually at least one night a week, guaranteed, where I end up doing this. But if I'm being completely honest with myself—and with you—it’s often two or three nights a week. \n\nIt almost always hits me after a late shift, or, you know, a really rough day on the line. The schedule at the plant has been absolutely brutal lately, doc. They’ve got us doing these mandatory overtime blocks, and when the machinery acts up—like last week, the main conveyor belt on line three kept seizing up—the foreman just gets so wound up, and that tension just trickles right down to all of us on the floor. You're standing there on the concrete for ten, twelve hours, just trying to keep the quota up. \n\nBy the time I finally punch out, my back is aching, my shoulders are practically up to my ears, and my brain is just totally fried. I'll get in my truck and I try to tell myself, 'Roy, just drive straight home, take a hot shower, and get right into bed. You need the sleep.' But then the stress of the day just sort of... settles into my bones on the drive back, you know? And before I know it, I'm pulling into the driveway, but I'm not going inside to my wife and my bed. I'm heading straight for the workshop and that garage fridge. \n\nSo yeah, it's been a steady two or three times a week for a few months now, just depending on how bad the shifts are. You tell yourself it's just a one-time thing to blow off steam because work was a nightmare, but then Tuesday becomes Thursday, and Thursday becomes Friday, and suddenly the weeks just start piling up and you realize it's become a whole routine.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or upset after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Oh, doc, guilty doesn't even begin to cover it. It's... it's this heavy, sinking feeling that just drops right into the pit of my stomach the second I take that last bite, or the second I finally force myself to stop. I'll be standing there in the cold garage, looking down at the empty chip bags, or the crumbs from those frosted cookies scattered all over the workbench, and this wave of... just absolute disgust washes over me. I feel so ashamed, I really do. \n\nYou see, I've worked at the plant for over twenty years now, right? Started on the floor right out of community college when I realized a desk job wasn't for me. And in all those years, I've built a reputation. Ask anyone on line three, ask the foreman—they'll tell you Roy is the guy you can count on. I show up early, I stay late, I don't complain, I get the job done. I've always prided myself on being disciplined. Being a dependable man, a provider for my wife, someone my grandkids can look up to. That's who I am. Or at least, that's who I thought I was.\n\nBut then I look at what I've just done... sneaking around in my own garage in the middle of the night, cramming food into my mouth like I'm out of my mind, hiding the wrappers at the bottom of the trash can so my wife doesn't see them when she takes the bags out in the morning. It makes me feel like a fraud. Like I'm living this double life. Here I am, this dependable guy during the day, but at night I'm sneaking around like a teenager trying not to get caught breaking curfew. I'm letting myself down, doc. Every single time I do it, I tell myself, 'Never again, Roy, that was the last time.' And then a few days later, I'm right back out there, doing it again, and the shame just piles up higher and higher until I feel like I can barely look at myself in the mirror when I'm shaving the next morning. It's just... it's a terrible way to live with yourself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Control? No. No, not at all. That's the scariest part of the whole thing, doc. It's like... well, it's exactly like someone else takes the wheel, you know? I'll be out there, standing by the workbench, and maybe I just went out to grab a bottle of water or put away a tool I left out from the weekend. But then I open that fridge, or I see the bag of chips I stashed behind the paint cans, and it's like... click. A switch just flips in my brain. \n\nOnce I start, once I take that first bite, the control is just gone. Completely gone. I'll actually be standing there, chewing, and my own mind is screaming at me. I'm literally telling myself, 'Roy, shut the bag. Just roll the top down, put it away, and go inside. Go to bed.' I hear the voice clear as day. But my hands... my hands just aren't listening to me. They just keep reaching into the bag, pulling out more, shoving it in. \n\nIt's the strangest, most helpless feeling in the world. You're a grown man, you've worked hard your whole life, you pride yourself on discipline, like I said before. I can stand on that factory floor for twelve hours and not take a break until the whistle blows, because I have control over my body and my work. But put me in front of that garage fridge at midnight after a bad shift, and I'm totally powerless. I'll watch my own hand reach for another cookie, and another, and I can't stop it. I just can't stop it until the box is empty or my stomach hurts so bad I physically can't swallow another bite. It's terrifying, honestly, to feel like you don't even have a say in what your own hands are doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's something specific that triggers these late-night eating episodes, like stress or anxiety from your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... you hit the nail right on the head there, doc. It is the stress. It is absolutely the stress from the plant. Like I was saying before, it is not just that the work is hard. I have done hard work my whole life, you know? Growing up, my old man had me out in the yard doing heavy lifting, fixing up old engines, so I know what physical labor is. I am not afraid of sweating. But this... this is different. It is this constant, grinding pressure. You are standing there on the line, and the conveyor is moving, and you know if you miss one piece, or if the machine calibration is off by even a fraction, the whole batch is ruined. And the foreman, he is always pacing up and down the catwalk, looking at his clipboard, checking his watch. You can practically feel his eyes burning a hole right in the back of your neck. \n\nSo yeah, it builds up this... this tight knot in my chest. I guess you could call it anxiety. I never really thought of myself as an anxious guy, I always thought of anxiety as something people get when they have to give a speech or something, but when I am driving home after a shift like that, my knuckles are completely white on the steering wheel. I grip it so hard my hands actually cramp up. I try turning on the radio, maybe listening to the sports talk station just to drown it all out, but my brain is still back at the factory, just replaying every single little thing that went wrong that day. Did I secure that pressure valve right? Is the morning crew going to complain about the scrap pile? It just spins and spins. \n\nAnd by the time I pull into my driveway, I am just so wound up that the idea of going inside and lying down in a quiet, dark bedroom... it just feels impossible. My mind is racing too fast for sleep. I need something to... to push it all down, I guess. To just numb it out and make the spinning stop. So I go straight to the garage. Eating all that junk, it is like it is the only thing that gives me a minute of quiet in my head, even if it makes me feel like absolute garbage afterward. \n\nSo, yeah, if you are asking if work is the trigger... I really do not know what else it could be. Because on my days off, if I am just puttering around the yard or watching the grandkids, I do not even look twice at the garage fridge. I just eat a normal dinner with my wife and that is that. It is always, always after those brutal shifts when my brain just will not shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel uncomfortably full after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Uncomfortable doesn't even begin to cover the half of it, doc. You know, when I'm in the middle of it, like I was saying before about that switch flipping in my brain, I don't really feel anything at all. It's like my stomach is just this bottomless pit while I'm standing there at the workbench shoving it all in. I don't register the fullness while it's happening. But then... then I finally stop. \n\nAnd by the time I actually do stop, my stomach is just... it's so incredibly tight against my work pants. I wear these heavy-duty canvas trousers to the plant, you know, the kind with the reinforced knees and a real stiff waistband because you need something durable when you're rubbing up against the machinery all day. Well, after one of these episodes, that waistband is digging into my gut so hard it actually leaves a deep red mark on my skin. I'll end up just sitting there on this old metal stool I keep by the tool chest, leaned all the way back against the drywall, just completely stuffed. \n\nIt gets so bad, I am uncomfortable enough that I don't want to move for a while. I literally can't. I just sit there, staring at the oil stains on the concrete floor, feeling like I swallowed a bowling ball. I can't bend over to pick up the wrappers I dropped, I can't stand up to reach the pull-string for the overhead fluorescent light... it takes a good twenty, maybe thirty minutes before I can even muster the energy or the physical ability to heave myself off that stool and make my way into the house. And then trying to sleep like that? Lying flat on my back in bed while my stomach feels like it's stretching right out of my skin, listening to my wife breathe and hoping I don't wake her up with how much I'm shifting around trying to find a position that doesn't physically hurt... it's just miserable. You'd think a rational man would remember that exact feeling of his pants cutting off his circulation and use it to stop himself the next time, right? You'd think I'd remember sitting on that stool in pain, but when that stress hits after a shift, it's like all that memory just gets wiped clean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what or how much you're eating during these episodes even when you want to stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, doc, exactly. Like I was trying to explain earlier, that is the exact feeling, and it's the part that drives me the craziest. It's just like I said about that switch flipping in my head—once that happens, what I actually *want* to do doesn't matter a lick. \n\nI can be standing there by the workbench, completely aware of what's happening, and my rational brain is fighting it the whole time. I'll be staring at the chips or maybe a container of leftover casserole I found in the back of the fridge, and I'm telling myself, over and over, 'Roy, just put the lid back on. Shut the bag, turn off the garage light, and just go inside to your wife.' I want to stop. I really, truly want to stop, especially knowing how bad my stomach is going to hurt later. But my hands... they just don't care what my brain wants. \n\nThey just keep reaching out, grabbing more, and bringing it to my mouth. It's like they're on some kind of automatic factory setting that I can't override, like a machine on the assembly line that just keeps punching out parts even when the conveyor belt is jammed. I can literally watch my own arm reaching back into the box for another handful, and I'm pleading with myself to just walk away, but I just cannot make the physical motion stop. My hands just keep going back for more, over and over, until the food is completely gone. It's the most frustrating thing in the world, being trapped in your own body like that, watching yourself do something you are actively trying to talk yourself out of.\n\nDoctor: Has your late-night eating affected your relationships with your family or your performance at work?\n\nPatient: Well, as far as the plant goes, no, I wouldn't say it's affecting my performance. I'm still getting to work on time every day, punching the clock, doing what needs to be done. I haven't missed a single shift, and my quotas are exactly where they need to be. And outside of work, you know, I'm still keeping up with church. My wife and I, we're in our same pew every Sunday morning, haven't missed a service. Pastor Jim would probably send a search party if we did, seeing as we've been going there for twenty-odd years. So, the outside world... I think I'm holding that together just fine. People looking at me probably think everything is completely normal.\n\nBut at home... yeah. It's definitely affecting things there. Not that we're yelling or fighting or anything like that, but... I've just been a lot more withdrawn. It’s just... well, it takes so much energy, doc. So much mental energy just trying to cover my tracks. \n\nLike I was saying about coming home from those shifts—if I get off a day shift and I stop at the convenience store on the way back, or I go straight into the garage and eat all that junk before I go inside... by the time I finally walk through the front door into the kitchen, my wife is usually just finishing up making dinner. And she’s a wonderful cook, she really is. She’ll have a nice pot roast or a meatloaf going, she's setting the table, asking me how my day was... and I’m standing there with my stomach already stretched to the bursting point like I described earlier. \n\nSo I have to stand there and lie to her. I have to make up all these excuses about why I’m not hungry for dinner. I’ll tell her, \"Oh, the guys brought in pizzas for a retirement thing late in the afternoon,\" or \"My stomach is just a little sour from the fumes at the plant today.\" And then I have to make sure I sneak out later and bury the wrappers deep in the outside trash bin so she doesn't find them when she takes the kitchen bag out. It's just exhausting. I spend all this energy keeping the secret, covering up where I stopped and what I did, that by the time I sit down in my recliner in the living room, I don't even want to talk. I just stare at the TV and give her one-word answers. I'm just so wrapped up in hiding it all that I'm completely pulling away from her in our own house.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant weight gain since these late-night eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, doc, because you would really think so, right? With the sheer amount of food I'm packing away out there by the workbench, you'd expect me to be buying new pants every other month. But honestly... no. I really haven't noticed any significant weight gain at all. \n\nI mean, I step on that old dial scale we've had in our master bathroom since we bought the place—the one with the little rubber mat that's starting to peel up at the corners—and the needle pretty much hovers right exactly where it always has for the last, oh, ten or fifteen years. I've always been a sturdy guy, you know, broad-shouldered, carrying a little extra around the middle, but my weight hasn't suddenly spiked or ballooned out of control since these late-night episodes started happening. \n\nI figure a lot of that has to do with the plant. People who work desk jobs don't always realize how physical production work actually is. Even when you're just monitoring the line, you are on your feet on that hard, unforgiving concrete for eight, sometimes ten or twelve hours if we're pulling overtime to meet a quota. You're constantly walking the floor, climbing up and down those steep metal catwalks to check the hoppers, hauling materials around... and just sweating buckets in the summer because the ventilation in that building is an absolute joke. So I guess I'm just burning a lot of those calories off just doing my day-to-day job. \n\nLike I was saying about those heavy canvas work trousers earlier—sure, they cut into my gut like a wire right after I finish eating all that junk in the garage, but by the time my alarm goes off at 4:30 the next morning and I'm getting dressed in the dark so I don't wake my wife, I button them up and they fit exactly the same as they always do. I'm still using the exact same notch on my leather belt that I've used for years. So, no, no real weight gain to speak of. Which I guess is a blessing, but it almost makes it more confusing, you know? Like my body is just hiding all the evidence of what I'm doing out there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel emotionally upset or experience guilt after these episodes of late-night eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, doc, that's... that's probably the hardest part to talk about, honestly. It really is. Because once that switch flips back, you know? Like I was saying earlier about my hands just moving on their own like an assembly line—well, eventually the line stops. The food is gone. And then I'm just standing there in the garage, usually under that one flickering fluorescent bulb over the workbench that I've been meaning to replace for six months, just staring down at a pile of empty wrappers or a scraped-clean Tupperware container. \n\nAnd the feeling that washes over me right then... it's just pure shame. I feel so ashamed, and honestly, just completely disgusted with myself. \n\nYou have to understand, I’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined guy. I’ve worked at the plant for decades, I provide for my wife, I show up to church every Sunday, I pay the mortgage on time. People rely on me. I’m dependable. If someone needs a shift covered, they call me. If a machine breaks down and needs a steady hand, they call me. I've built my whole life on being the guy who holds it together, the guy who does what he's supposed to do, when he's supposed to do it. I've never been one to lose control.\n\nSo to be standing out there in the dark, wiping grease off my chin and hiding evidence from my own wife... it makes me feel like I’m just sneaking around like some kind of thief in my own home. It’s pathetic, is what it is. I feel like I'm completely letting myself down. I just stand there looking at the mess and think, 'What kind of a man does this at your age?' It completely shatters how I see myself. I mean, how can I be this dependable, rock-solid guy out in the world, but I can't even walk past a box of crackers in my own garage without losing my mind? It just eats away at me. I'll just sit out there on my work stool sometimes afterward, before I finally go inside to face my wife, just feeling sick to my stomach—and not just from the sheer amount of food, but from the disgust. It just makes me feel so small."
